\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\beta}T^{\alpha}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2(n-l)}}T^{\beta}_{j,i_{2(n-l)+1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}w^{i}w^{j}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}w^{i}w^{j}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}},

where the last equality follows simply by re-ordering the indices. Inserting this identity into
Eq.

F.12
, since
w
was arbitrary, we obtain that for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}

\displaystyle\begin{split}(2n+2)(2n+1)g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{2n+1}\binom{2n+2}{l}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}\\
&=2^{2n+1}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}.\end{split}

(F.13)

∎

In the following proposition, we will obtain a similar expression for the derivatives of the coefficients of the inverse metric in normal coordinates. Although this will not be used to study the Laplacian or the gradient of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
, it will play an essential role in
Section

F.4.2
.

Proposition F.10
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete and connected
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold which is furthermore a symmetric space, and let
p\in M
be some fixed point in
M
. For any
x\in M
outside the cut locus of
p
, taking normal coordinates centered at
p
, we denote the coefficients of the inverse metric
g^{-1}
at
x
as
g^{ij}(x)
.

Let
(g^{(k)}(0))^{ij}
be the analogous of
g^{(k)}_{ij}(0)
, i.e.

(g^{(k)}(0))^{ij}:=\left.\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}\right|_{t=0}g^{ij}(\gamma(t)),

where
\gamma(t)=\exp_{p}(t\log_{p}x)
. Then for every
n\geq 0
and every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
it holds that

\displaystyle(g^{(2n+1)}(0))^{ij}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\big(g^{(2n)}(0)\big)^{ij}

\displaystyle=C_{2n}\delta^{i\beta}T^{j}_{\beta,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{2n}},

where

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle:=1,

\displaystyle C_{2n}

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}C_{2(n-m)}\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+3)}.

Proof.

By definition of the inverse metric, it holds that

g_{ik}(\gamma(t))g^{kj}(\gamma(t))=\delta_{i}^{j},\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}.

Taking the differential with respect to
t
, at
t=0
, we conclude that

g^{(1)}_{ik}(0)g^{kj}(\gamma(0))+g_{ik}(\gamma(0))(g^{(1)}(0))^{kj}=0,\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}.

(F.14)

Now, recall that
g_{ik}(\gamma(0))=\delta_{ik}
and
g^{(1)}_{ik}(0)=0
for every
i,k\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
, which allows us to rewrite
Eq.

F.14
as

\delta_{ik}(g^{(1)}(0))^{kj}=0,\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}.

Therefore, we conclude that

(g^{(1)}(0))^{ij}=0,\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}.

We can now prove that

(g^{(2n+1)}(0))^{ij}=0,\quad\forall n\geq 0,\ \forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}

by induction. Indeed, for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
using the general Leibniz rule it holds that

0=\left.\frac{d^{2n-1}}{dt^{2n-1}}\right|_{t=0}\Big(g_{ik}(\gamma(t))g^{kj}(\gamma(t))\Big)=\sum_{m=0}^{2n-1}\binom{2n-1}{m}g^{(2n-1-m)}_{ik}(0)(g^{(m)}(0))^{kj}.

(F.15)

In every summand on the right-hand side of this identity either
m
or
2n-1-m
is odd, and so by induction hypothesis, using the fact that
g^{(2n-1)}_{ij}(0)=0
, for every
n\geq 1
(cf.
Eq.

F.11
) we can rewrite
Eq.

F.15
as

0=\delta_{ik}(g^{(2n+1)}(0))^{kj},

and the claim follows.

Let us now consider the even derivatives of the inverse metric coefficients. We proceed by induction. For
n=0
, it holds that

\delta^{i}_{j}=\big(g(0)\big)^{ik}\delta_{kj},\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\},

which allows us to conclude that

\big(g(0)\big)^{ij}=\delta^{ij},\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}.

On the other hand,

C_{0}\delta^{i\beta}T^{j}_{\beta}=\delta^{i\beta}\delta^{j}_{\beta}=\delta^{ij},\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\},

which proves the base case.

Now, for
n\geq 1
, using the general Leibniz rule, we know that for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
,

\displaystyle(g^{(2n)}(0))^{ik}\delta_{kj}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}(g^{2(n-m)}(0))^{ik}g_{kj}^{(2m)}(0)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}(g^{2(n-m)}(0))^{ik}\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+1)}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{k,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2m}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2m}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}C_{2(n-m)}\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+1)}\delta^{i\beta}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{k}_{\beta,j_{1},\dotsc,j_{2(n-m)}}T^{\alpha}_{k,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2m}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{2(n-m)}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}},

where the second equality follows from
Proposition

F.9
and the third equality follows from the induction hypothesis.

By simply reordering the indices, it holds that

T^{k}_{\beta,j_{1},\dotsc,j_{2(n-m)}}T^{\alpha}_{k,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2m}}x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{2(n-m)}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2m}}=T^{\alpha}_{\beta,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}},

and so we can write

\displaystyle(g^{(2n)}(0))^{ik}\delta_{kj}=-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}C_{2(n-m)}\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+1)}\delta^{i\beta}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{\beta,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}},

for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. The result now follows by rewriting this expression as

(g^{(2n)}(0))^{ij}=-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}C_{2(n-m)}\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+1)}\delta^{i\beta}T^{j}_{\beta,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}.

and defining

C_{2n}:=-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}C_{2(n-m)}\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+1)}.

∎

F.2.2
Bounding the Laplacian

Having obtained an explicit expression for the Taylor series of the coefficients of the metric
g
i normal coordinates, we now proceed to upper bound the absolute value of
g_{ij}(x)
and
\partial_{a}g_{ij}(x)
for every
a,i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. By bounding the former, we will be able to upper bound the absolute value of the coefficients of the inverse metric, which will allow us to upper bound the absolute value of the Christoffel symbols, and ultimately
|\Delta_{g}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|
.

Proposition F.11
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete and connected
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold which is also a symmetric space. Let
p\in M
be a fixed point and let us consider normal coordinates centered at
p
. Let
0<r<i(M)
and let
x\in M
be such that
d_{g}(x,p)\leq r
. Assume that, at
p
, there exists some constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor in these coordinates satisfy
|R_{ijk}^{l}(p)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. Then

\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!}g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)\right|\leq\frac{2}{d}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d^{3n}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2r)^{2n}}{(2n+2)!},

for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
.

Proof.

In
Proposition

F.9
we showed that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)=\frac{2^{2n+1}}{(2n+2)(2n+1)}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}},\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}.

It is now straightforward to obtain a bound for this expression. As in normal coordinates centered at
p
it holds that
|R_{ijk}^{l}(p)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
, it follows that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

|T^{\alpha}_{\beta i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}|\leq d^{n-1}\mathbf{K}^{n},\quad\forall\alpha,\beta,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}.

(F.16)

Since we are also assuming that
d_{g}(p,x)=|\log_{p}x|_{g}\leq r
, it follows that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(2n+2)(2n+1)|g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2n+1}d^{2n}d^{n-1}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n}

\displaystyle=2^{2n+1}d^{3n-1}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n},

for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
.

This way, we proved that for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}

\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!}g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)\right|\leq\frac{2}{d}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d^{3n}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2r)^{2n}}{(2n+2)!},

finishing the proof.
∎

Proposition

F.11
allows us to obtain bounds which can be as small as desired, simply by requiring
r
to be small enough.

Remark F.12
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition

F.11
, let

r\leq\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}^{1/2}}.

Then

\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!}g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)\right|<\frac{1}{d}.

Proof.

By simply inserting the bound on
r
into the expression found in
Proposition

F.11
we see that

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!}g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{d}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d^{3n}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2r)^{2n}}{(2n+2)!}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{d}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d^{3n}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}^{1/2}})^{2n}}{(2n+2)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{d}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{4^{n}}{(2n+2)!}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d}.

∎

As we mentioned earlier, we need to find an upper bound on the absolute value of the coefficients of
g^{-1}
at
x
in normal coordinates, as they appear both in the expression of the Christoffel symbols and in the Laplacian of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
. In order to do so, we will use two auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma F.13
.

For every matrix
A
of size
n\times m
it holds that

\max_{ij}|a_{ij}|\leq\norm{A}_{F}\leq\sqrt{nm}\max_{ij}|a_{ij}|,

where
\norm{A}_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm of
A
, namely

\norm{A}_{F}:=\Tr(A^T A)=\Big(\sum_{ij}|a_{ij}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

The next lemma will allow us to obtain a Taylor series expansion for the coefficients of
g^{-1}
in normal coordinates using what is known as a Neumann series approximation. We refer the reader to
[
73
, Theorem 4.20]
for more details.

Lemma F.14
.

Let
X
be some matrix such that
\norm{X}<1
for some matrix norm
\norm{\cdot}
. Then
(\mathds{1}-X)
is non-singular and

(\mathds{1}-X)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}X^{k}.

As we mentioned previously, these lemmas allow us to prove the following result, which gives us a bound on the absolute value of the coefficients of
g^{-1}
in normal coordinates.

Proposition F.15
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete and connected
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold which is also a symmetric space. Let
p\in M
be a fixed point and let us consider normal coordinates centered at
p
. Let
0<r<i(M)
and let
x\in M
be such that
d_{g}(x,p)\leq r
. Assume that, at
p
, there exists some constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor in these coordinates satisfy
|R_{ijk}^{l}(p)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. Further assume that

r\leq\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}^{1/2}}.

Then for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
,

|g^{ij}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{1-L},

where

L:=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d^{3n}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2r)^{2n}}{(2n+2)!}.

(F.17)

Proof.

First, we can write
g
as

g(x)=\mathds{1}+X,

where

X_{ij}=g_{ij}-\delta_{ij}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)}{2n!},

for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
.

From
Proposition

F.11
we can conclude that for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
,

|X_{ij}|=|g_{ij}-\delta_{ij}|\leq\frac{L}{d},

where
L
is as defined in
Eq.

F.17
. Therefore, by
Remarks

F.12
and
F.13
we can conclude that

\norm{X}_{F}\leq d\max_{ij}|X_{ij}|\leq L<1,

(F.18)

and so by
Lemma

F.14
the metric
g
is non-singular and

g^{-1}=(\mathds{1}+X)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}X^{k},

which implies that

\norm{g^{-1}}_{F}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\norm{X}^{k}_{F}\leq\frac{1}{1-L}.

(F.19)

Lastly, we can use
Lemma

F.13
again to guarantee that for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}

|g^{ij}(x)|\leq\max_{ij}|g^{ij}(x)|\leq\norm{g^{-1}}_{F}\leq\frac{1}{1-L}.

∎

To finish, we will follow the same steps as in
Proposition

F.11
to give an upper bound on
|\partial_{a}g_{ij}(x)|
in normal coordinates centered at
p
, depending on
d_{g}(x,p)
and the bound on the Riemann curvature tensor.

Proposition F.16
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete and connected
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold which is also a symmetric space. Let
p\in M
be a fixed point and let us consider normal coordinates centered at
p
. Let
0<r<i(M)
and let
x\in M
be such that
d_{g}(x,p)\leq r
. Assume that, at
p
, there exists some constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor in these coordinates satisfy
|R_{ijk}^{l}(p)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. Then

|\partial_{a}g_{ij}(x)|\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n2^{2n+2}d^{3n-2}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n-1}}{(2n+2)!},

for every
a,i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
.

Proof.

For every
a,i\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
, we can write

\partial_{a}x^{i}=\delta^{i}_{a}.

Therefore, if we take the derivative with respect to the
a
-th coordinate in
Eq.

F.13
we obtain that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}

(2n+2)(2n+1)\partial_{a}g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)=2^{2n+1}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}_{a}

where

x^{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}_{a}:=\delta^{i_{1}}_{a}x^{i_{2}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}+x^{i_{1}}\delta^{i_{2}}_{a}x^{i_{3}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}+\dotsm+x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n-1}}\delta^{i_{2n}}_{a}.

The norm of these elements can be bounded as

\displaystyle|x^{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}_{a}|

\displaystyle\leq|\delta^{i_{1}}_{a}x^{i_{2}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}|+|x^{i_{1}}\delta^{i_{2}}_{a}x^{i_{3}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n}}|+\dotsm+|x^{i_{1}}\dotsm x^{i_{2n-1}}\delta^{i_{2n}}_{a}|

\displaystyle\leq 2nr^{2n-1}.

Therefore, using this inequality together with
Eq.

F.16
we conclude that for every
n\geq 1
and every
a,i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
,

\displaystyle(2n+2)(2n+1)|\partial_{a}g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)|

\displaystyle=|2^{2n+1}\delta_{\alpha j}T^{\alpha}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}_{a}|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2n+1}|T^{j}_{i,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}||x^{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}_{a}|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2n+1}d^{2n-1}d^{n-1}\mathbf{K}^{n}2nr^{2n-1}

\displaystyle=2^{2n+2}nd^{3n-2}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n-1},

and so

\displaystyle|\partial_{a}g_{ij}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!}|\partial_{a}g^{(2n)}_{ij}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n2^{2n+2}d^{3n-2}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n-1}}{(2n+2)!},

for every
a,i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
.
∎

Having obtained a bound on
|\partial_{a}g_{ij}(x)|
and
|g^{ij}(x)|
for every
a,i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
, the bound on the Laplacian of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
follows almost immediately, as we will show in the next theorem. Note that the bound for the absolute value of the Laplacian can be made arbitrarily small by shrinking
r
. Nevertheless, the bound we obtain below is sufficiently good for the purpose of this work.

Proposition F.17

(Upper bound on the absolute value of the Laplacian of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete and connected
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold, which is also a symmetric space. Let
p\in M
be a fixed point and let us consider normal coordinates centered at
p
. Let
0<r<i(M)
and let
x\in M
be such that
d_{g}(x,p)\leq r
. Assume that, at
p
, there exists some constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor in these coordinates satisfy
|R_{ijk}^{l}(p)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. Then if

r\leq\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}},

it holds that

|\Delta_{g}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|\leq 24d^{5/2}.

Proof.

In normal coordinates, the Christoffel symbols can be written as

\Gamma^{1}_{kl}(x)=\frac{1}{2}g^{1m}(x)\Big(\partial_{l}g_{mk}(x)+\partial_{k}g_{ml}(x)-\partial_{m}g_{kl}(x)\Big),

for every
k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. Thus, we can rewrite
Eq.

F.3
as

\Delta_{g}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)=-g^{ij}(x)\Gamma^{1}_{ij}(x)=-\frac{1}{2}g^{ij}(x)g^{1k}(x)(\partial_{j}g_{ki}(x)+\partial_{i}g_{kj}(x)-\partial_{k}g_{ij}(x)).

(F.20)

By
Proposition

F.16
, we know that for every
a,i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
,

|\partial_{a}g_{ij}(x)|\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n2^{2n+2}d^{3n-2}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n-1}}{(2n+2)!}.

Therefore, since

r\leq\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}},

and since we are assuming without loss of generality that
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
, we see that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\partial_{a}g_{ij}(x)|&\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n2^{2n+2}d^{3n-2}\mathbf{K}^{n}(\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}})^{2n-1}}{(2n+2)!}\\
&=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n2^{2n+2}d^{-1/2}\mathbf{K}^{-n+1}}{(2n+2)!}\\
&\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n4^{n}}{(2n+2)!}\\
&\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{d}}.\end{split}

(F.21)

Now, we can use
Proposition

F.15
to conclude that for every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}

|g^{ij}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{1-L},

where
L
is as defined in
Eq.

F.17
.

Again, using that

r\leq\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}}\leq\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}^{1/2}},

we can conclude that

L\leq 2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{4^{n}}{(2n+2)!}<\frac{1}{2}.

and so

|g^{ij}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{2}}=2.

(F.22)

Inserting
Eqs.

F.21
and
F.22
into
Eq.

F.20
it follows that

|\Delta_{g}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|\leq 24d^{5/2},

concluding the proof.
∎

F.3
Bounding the norm of the gradient in symmetric spaces

To finish, let us focus on the gradient of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)
, for which we will obtain a lower and an upper bound on its norm. To this end, we will use the bounds on the metric derived in the previous section.

Lemma F.18
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete and connected Riemannian manifold which is also a symmetric space of dimension
d
. Let
p\in M
be a fixed point and let us consider normal coordinates centered at
p
. Let
0<r<i(M)
and let
x\in M
be such that
d_{g}(x,p)\leq r
. In these coordinates, the gradient of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
can be written as

\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)=g^{ij}(x)\delta_{ik}v^{k}\partial_{j},

where
v=v^{i}\partial_{i}\in T_{p}M
is the vector with respect to which
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
is defined.

Proof.

In normal coordinates centered at
p
,
\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)=\langle v,x\rangle=\delta_{ij}v^{i}x^{j}
. Recall that, in these coordinates,

\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)=g^{ij}(x)\frac{\partial\tilde{r}(x)}{\partial x^{i}}\partial_{j}.

Therefore, using that
\partial_{a}x^{i}=\delta_{a}^{i}
for every
a,i\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
it holds that

\partial_{a}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)=\delta_{ai}v^{i},

and so

\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)=g^{ij}(x)\delta_{ik}v^{k}\partial_{j}.

∎

Proposition F.19
.

Under the conditions of
Lemma

F.18
, assume that there exists a constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that for every
x\in M
, in normal coordinates centered at
x
, the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor are such that
|R^{l}_{ijk}(x)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. If

r\leq\frac{1}{d^{3/2}\mathbf{K}^{1/2}},

then

\frac{1}{1+L}\leq|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|^{2}_{g}\leq\frac{1}{1-L},

where

L=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d^{3n}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2r)^{2n}}{(2n+2)!}.

Proof.

In
Lemma

F.18
we showed that

\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)=g^{ij}(x)\delta_{ik}v^{k}\partial_{j},

and so

|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|^{2}_{g}=g_{ij}(x)g^{ai}(x)\delta_{ab}v^{b}g^{cj}(x)\delta_{cd}v^{d}=\delta_{jb}\delta_{cd}g^{cj}(x)v^{b}v^{d}=\sum_{ij}g^{ij}(x)v^{i}v^{j}.

This expression can be easily lower bounded,

\displaystyle|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|^{2}_{g}=\sum_{ij}g^{ij}(x)v^{i}v^{j}\geq\lambda_{\min}(g^{-1}(x))|v|_{g}^{2}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\max}(g(x))},

as
|v|_{g}^{2}=1
.

Note that, as
g
is symmetric and positive-definite, its singular values correspond to its eigenvalues, and so

\norm{g}_{2}:=\sigma_{\max}(g)=\lambda_{\max}(g),

where
\sigma_{\max}(g)
denotes the greatest singular value of
g
and
\lambda_{\max}(g)
denotes its greatest eigenvalue. Moreover, this norm is upper bounded by the Frobenius norm from
Lemma

F.13
, which can be written as

\norm{g}_{F}=\sqrt{\sigma_{1}(g)^{2}+\dotsc+\sigma_{d}(g)^{2}}=\sqrt{\lambda_{1}(g)^{2}+\dotsc+\lambda_{d}(g)^{2}},

and so
\norm{g}_{2}\leq\norm{g}_{F}
. Thus, using
Eq.

F.18
we can conclude that

\lambda_{\max}(g(x))=\norm{g}_{2}=\norm{\mathds{1} + X}_{2}\leq\norm{1}_{2}+\norm{X}_{2}\leq 1+\norm{X}_{F}\leq 1+L,

and so

|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|^{2}_{g}\geq\frac{1}{1+L}.

Similarly,

|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(x)|^{2}_{g}\leq\lambda_{\max}(g^{-1}(x))=\norm{g^{-1}(x)}_{2}\leq\norm{g^{-1}(x)}_{F}\leq\frac{1}{1-L},

where the last inequality follows from
Eq.

F.19
.
∎

F.4
Two auxiliary lemmas

We now state and prove the auxiliary lemmas
F.22
and
F.20
used in
Section

2.2.2
. We begin with
Lemma

F.20
, as its proof is simpler.

Let us recall the framework in which the lemmas are formulated. Let
\pi:(M,g)\to(B,h)
be a Riemannian submersion with totally geodesic fibers, where
(M,g)
is furthermore a complete and connected symmetric space. Let us fix some
p\in B
, and define the following two functions for every
q\in B
outside the cut locus of
p
,

\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)=\langle v,\log_{p}q\rangle,

and

H_{p}(q)=\nabla^{2}\tilde{F}(p)[\log_{p}q],

where
\tilde{F}
is some suitable smooth function on
B
, and
v\in T_{p}B
is some fixed tangent vector.

To analyze the above functions, we will
lift
them from
B
to
M
, study their lifted versions, and then
transfer
the properties of the lifted functions to the original ones using the results from
Section

A.5
.

F.4.1
First auxiliary lemma

Lemma F.20
.

Let
\pi:(M,g)\to(B,h)
be a surjective Riemannian submersion with totally geodesic fibers, where
(M,g)
is a compact and connected symmetric space. Assume that there exists some constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that, for every
x\in M
, the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor in normal coordinates centered at
x
satisfy
|R_{ijk}^{l}(x)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,\dim(M)\}
. Let

r\leq\min\{i(M),i(B),\frac{1}{72\dim(M)^{5/2}\mathbf{K}}\}.

Let
p\in B
be some fixed point of
B
, and let
v\in T_{p}B
be a unitary tangent vector at
p
. Let us consider

\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)=\langle v,\log_{p}q\rangle,

for every
q\in B
outside the cut locus of
p
. Then it holds that

\frac{10}{11}\leq|\textup{grad}_{h}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)|^{2}_{h}\leq\frac{10}{9},

and

|\textup{grad}_{h}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)|^{2}_{h}+\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\Delta_{g}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\geq\frac{1}{2},

for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(r,p)
.

As we mentioned earlier, to obtain the above bounds, we will first obtain a lifted version of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
, which we will denote
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
. We will then use the results from
Sections

F.2
and
F.3
to obtain bounds for the norm of the gradient and the Laplacian of
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
, using the fact that
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
is defined on a symmetric space. Lastly, we will obtain the desired bounds for
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
using
Section

A.5
.

In the following proposition we explicitly construct the function which is the
lifted
version of
\tilde{r}_{p,v}
.

To clarify the notation, we will use
\log^{B}
and
\log^{M}
throughout the subsection for the inverses of the exponential maps in
B
and
M
, respectively.

Proposition F.21
.

Let
\pi:(M,g)\to(B,h)
be a surjective Riemannian submersion. Let
p\in B
and let
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
be fixed. Let
\alpha\leq\min\{i(M),i(B)\}
. Then for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,p)
and every
x,y\in\pi^{-1}(q)\cap\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
, it holds that

(\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}}=(\log^{M}_{z}y)^{\mathcal{H}},

and

\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}})=\log_{p}^{B}q.

In particular, given a fixed point
p\in B
, it holds that for every
v\in T_{p}B
and every
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
the functions

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{p,v}:\mathcal{B}(\alpha,p)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\quad\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}:\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle v,\log^{B}_{p}q\rangle

\displaystyle\quad x

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\textup{lift}_{z}v,\log^{M}_{z}x\rangle,

are such that
\tilde{r}_{p,v}(\pi(x))=\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)
for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
.

Proof.

Given
p\in B
and
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
, let us fix some
q\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,p)
and let us consider two points
x,y\in\pi^{-1}(q)\cap\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
. First of all, recall that
\pi\circ\exp^{M}_{z}=\exp^{B}_{p}\circ\,\pi_{*}|_{z}
(cf.
Lemma

A.68
), and so

\exp^{B}_{p}(\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}}))=\exp^{B}_{p}(\pi_{*}(\log^{M}_{z}x))=\pi(\exp_{z}^{M}(\log^{M}_{z}x))=\pi(x)=q.

Following the same reasoning, we also obtain that

\exp^{B}_{p}(\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}y)^{\mathcal{H}}))=q,

so both
\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}})
and
\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}y)^{\mathcal{H}})
are tangent vectors at
p
which get mapped to
q
via the exponential map.

Moreover, from
\pi
being a Riemannian submersion, it holds that

\displaystyle|\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}})|_{h}

\displaystyle=|(\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}}|_{g}\leq|\log^{M}_{z}x|_{g}\leq\alpha\leq i(B),

\displaystyle|\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}y)^{\mathcal{H}})|_{h}

\displaystyle=|(\log^{M}_{z}y)^{\mathcal{H}}|_{g}\leq|\log^{M}_{z}y|_{g}\leq\alpha\leq i(B),

and so from
\exp^{B}_{p}
being a local diffeomorphism in the ball of radius
i(B)
, it has to be the case that

\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}})=\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}y)^{\mathcal{H}}),

which in turn allows us to conclude that

(\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}}=(\log^{M}_{z}y)^{\mathcal{H}},

and

\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}})=\log^{B}_{p}q.

Given some fixed
p\in B
, and any
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
it holds that

\pi(\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z))=\mathcal{B}(\alpha,x),

as we saw in
Proposition

A.34
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{p,v}(\pi(x))=\langle v,\log^{B}_{p}\pi(x)\rangle=\langle v,\pi_{*}((\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\textup{lift}_{z}v,(\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\textup{lift}_{z}v,\log^{M}_{z}x\rangle

\displaystyle=\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x),

so the result follows.
∎

With the previous lemma in mind, we are ready to prove
Lemma

F.20
.

Proof of
Lemma

F.20
.

Given some
p\in B
fixed, let us fix some
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
. As we are assuming that

r\leq\min\{i(M),i(B)\}
, and using
Proposition

F.21
we obtain a function
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
defined on
\mathcal{B}(r,z)
satisfying
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}=\tilde{r}_{p,v}\circ\pi
.

Moreover, by
Propositions

A.58
and
A.65
we know that their Laplacians and their gradients are related by the following identities;

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)&=\Delta_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(\pi(x)),\\
|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)|_{g}&=|\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(\pi(x))|_{h},\end{split}

(F.23)

for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(r,z)
.

As we are assuming that

r\leq\frac{1}{72\dim(M)^{5/2}\mathbf{K}},

we can apply
Propositions

F.17
and
F.19
to
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
to conclude that for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(r,z)
it holds that

\displaystyle|\Delta_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq 24\dim(M)^{5/2},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+L}\leq|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)|^{2}_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-L}.

Thus, inserting these bounds on
Eq.

F.23
we can conclude that

\displaystyle|\Delta_{h}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)|

\displaystyle\leq 24\text{dim}(M)^{5/2},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+L}\leq|\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)|^{2}_{h}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-L}.

for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(r,p)
.

Since
r\leq\frac{1}{2\text{dim}(M)^{3/2}\mathbf{K}}
, and
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
, we can bound
L
as

L\leq 2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{K}^{-n}}{(2n+2)!}\leq 2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2n+2)!}\leq\frac{1}{10},

and so

\frac{10}{11}\leq\frac{1}{1+L}\leq|\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)|^{2}_{h}\leq\frac{1}{1-L}\leq\frac{10}{9},

for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(r,p)
.

Lastly, note that
\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\leq d_{g}(p,q)
for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(r,p)
. Thus, since
r\leq\frac{1}{72\dim(M)^{5/2}}
by assumption, it holds that

|\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)|^{2}_{h}+\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\Delta_{h}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\geq\frac{10}{11}-\frac{1}{3}\geq\frac{1}{2},

for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(r,p)
.
∎

F.4.2
Second auxiliary Lemma

Lemma F.22
.

Let
\pi:(M,g)\to(B,h)
be a surjective Riemannian submersion, where
(M,g)
is a compact and connected symmetric space. Assume that there exists some constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that, for every
x\in M
, the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor in normal coordinates centered at
x
satisfy
|R_{ijk}^{l}(x)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,\dim(M)\}
.

Let
F
be a smooth function on
M
verifying
2.10
and
2.12
, and let
\tilde{F}
be the unique function on
B
satisfying
F=\tilde{F}\circ\pi
. Assume that
\tilde{F}
satisfies
2.13
to
2.17
. Let

r\leq\min\Bigg\{i(M),i(B),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\dim(M)^{3/2}\mathbf{K}}\Bigg\}.

Let
p\in B
be a fixed saddle point of
\tilde{F}
and let
v
be the unitary eigenvector of
\nabla^{2}\tilde{F}(p)
that corresponds to its minimum eigenvalue. Defining
\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)
with respect to
v
, i.e.

\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)=\langle v,\log^{B}_{p}q\rangle,

it holds that

\langle-H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}\geq\lambda_{*}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)-4\dim(M)^{5}A_{2}\mathbf{K}r^{3},

for every
q\in B\big(y,r\big)
, where

H_{p}(q):=\nabla^{2}_{h}\,\tilde{F}(p)[\log^{B}_{p}q],

and
A_{2}
is the Lipschitz constant of the gradient of
\tilde{F}
.

We proceed in the same manner as in the previous section; to prove
Lemma

F.22
we will
lift
the expression

\langle-H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}

(F.24)

to the total space
(M,g)
. We will obtain bounds for the lifted expression, using the fact that
(M,g)
is assumed to be a symmetric space, and then
transfer
the bounds to the original expression.

In the previous subsection we saw that, given some fixed
p\in B
, we can define a function
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
on
\mathcal{B}(r,z)
, for any
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
, when
r
is sufficiently small, satisfying
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}=\tilde{r}_{p,v}\circ\pi
. In the same spirit, we can also
lift
the function
H_{p}(q)
.

Lemma F.23
.

In the same setting as for
Lemma

F.22
, let
p\in B
be a fixed saddle point of
\tilde{F}
, and let
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
. Let

H^{M}_{z}(x):=\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\log^{M}_{z}x]

be defined in
\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
, where
\alpha=\min\{i(M),i(B)\}
. Then
H^{M}_{z}
is invariant in the fibers of
\pi
, i.e. for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,p)
and every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\pi^{-1}(q)\cap\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
, it holds that

H^{M}_{z}(x_{1})=H^{M}_{z}(x_{2}),

and

\pi_{*}H^{M}_{z}(x)=H_{p}(\pi(x)),

for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
.

Proof.

Note that
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
is a critical point of
F
by
Proposition

A.58
. Thus, we can apply
Proposition

A.62
to conclude that

H^{M}_{z}(x)=\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\log^{M}_{z}x]=\nabla^{2}_{g}\ F(z)[(\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}}],\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z).

Now, for every
q\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,p)
and every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\pi^{-1}(q)\cap\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
, we proved in
Proposition

F.21
that

(\log^{M}_{z}x_{1})^{\mathcal{H}}=(\log^{M}_{z}x_{2})^{\mathcal{H}},

and so
H^{M}_{z}(x_{1})=H^{M}_{z}(x_{2})
.

Moreover,

\displaystyle(H^{M}_{z}(x))^{\mathcal{H}}=(\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\log^{M}_{z}x])^{\mathcal{H}}=(\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[(\log^{M}_{z}x)^{\mathcal{H}}])^{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\text{lift}_{z}\nabla^{2}_{h}\,\tilde{F}(p)[\pi_{*}\log^{M}_{z}x]

\displaystyle=\text{lift}_{z}\nabla^{2}_{h}\,\tilde{F}(p)[\log^{B}_{p}q]

\displaystyle=\text{lift}_{z}\,H_{p}(q),

where the third-last identity follows from
Proposition

A.61
and the second-last identity follows from
Proposition

F.21
. Thus, it holds that

\pi_{*}H^{M}_{z}(x)=H_{p}(\pi(x)),

for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
, finishing the proof.
∎

Lemma

F.23
allows us to relate the expression shown in
Eq.

F.24
to its
lifted version
.

Lemma F.24
.

In the same setting as for
Lemma

F.22
, let
p\in B
be a fixed saddle point of
\tilde{F}
, and let
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
be fixed. Then for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
it holds that

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}=\langle H^{M}_{z}(x)^{\mathcal{H}},(\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))^{\mathcal{H}}\rangle_{g},

where
q=\pi(x)
.

Proof.

Given
p\in B
and
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
, let
x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
and let us denote its projection as
q:=\pi(x)
. First, we can use
Proposition

A.58
along with
Propositions

F.21
and
F.23
to rewrite

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}=\langle\pi_{*}\,H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\circ\pi_{*}(\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))\rangle_{h}.

Now, we will make use of
Lemma

A.68
, which guarantees that

\pi_{*}|_{z}\circ\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}=\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}{q}}\circ\pi_{*}|_{x}.

Therefore, we can commute the parallel transport and the pushforward, to obtain

\displaystyle\langle\pi_{*}\,H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\circ\pi_{*}(\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))\rangle_{h}

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{*}\,H^{M}_{z}(x),\pi_{*}\,\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)\rangle_{h}

\displaystyle=\langle H^{M}_{z}(x)^{\mathcal{H}},(\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))^{\mathcal{H}}\rangle_{g},

where the last equality follows from
\pi
being a Riemannian submersion.
∎

Now, in order to study
\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log_{z}^{M}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)
, we can use the following Taylor expansion for the parallel transport (cf.
[
70
, Section 6.9]
),

\displaystyle\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left.\nabla^{(n)}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left.\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t)),

where
\gamma(t)=\exp_{z}(t\log^{M}_{z}x)
. Using this expression for the parallel transport of the gradient of
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
and
Lemma

F.24
, we can obtain a lower bound on
Eq.

F.24
.

Lemma F.25
.

In the same setting as for
Lemma

F.22
, let
p\in B
be a fixed saddle point of
\tilde{F}
, and let
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
be fixed. Then for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
it holds that

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}\geq\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z))\rangle_{g}-2|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}|u|_{g},

where
q=\pi(x)
and
u:=\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}(\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))-\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)
.

Proof.

First, we can use
Lemma

F.24
to rewrite

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}=\langle H^{M}_{z}(x)^{\mathcal{H}},(\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))^{\mathcal{H}}\rangle_{g}.

(F.25)

Now, using the decomposition of the tangent space
T_{z}M
into the vertical and horizontal subspaces, we can rewrite the right-hand side of
Eq.

F.25
as

\displaystyle\langle H^{M}_{z}(x)^{\mathcal{H}},(\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))^{\mathcal{H}}\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle=\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle\quad-\langle H^{M}_{z}(x)^{\mathcal{V}},(\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))^{\mathcal{V}}\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle\quad-|H^{M}_{z}(x)^{\mathcal{V}}|_{g}|\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))^{\mathcal{V}}|_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)\rangle_{g}-|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}|u|_{g},

where the last inequality follows since the first term in the Taylor series of the parallel transport corresponds to
\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)
, which is horizontal by
Proposition

A.58
.

Lastly, we can again use the fact that

\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)=\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)+u,

to conclude that

\displaystyle\langle H^{M}_{z}(x)^{\mathcal{H}},(\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x))^{\mathcal{H}}\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)\rangle_{g}-|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}|u|_{g}

\displaystyle=\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z))\rangle_{g}+\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),u\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle\quad-|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}|u|_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z))\rangle_{g}-2|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}|u|_{g}.

∎

To obtain a more explicit bound, we need to upper bound the norm of
u
. To do this, we will study the derivatives of
\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
along the geodesic
\gamma(t)=\exp_{z}(t\log^{M}_{z}x)
.

From the definition of
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
, and the expression for its gradient given in
Lemma

F.18
, we know that in normal coordinates centered at
p
,

\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))=g^{ij}(\gamma(t))\delta_{ik}(\text{lift}_{z}v)^{k}\partial_{j},

and so

\left.\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))=(g^{(n)}(0))^{ij}\delta_{ik}(\text{lift}_{z}v)^{k}\partial_{j},

where
(g^{(n)}(0))^{ij}
is as defined in
Proposition

F.10
, i.e.

(g^{(n)}(0))^{ij}=\left.\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}\right|_{t=0}g^{ij}(\gamma(t)).

Therefore, the derivatives of the gradient of
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
along
\gamma(t)
, can be studied by considering the derivatives of the coefficients of the inverse metric along the same geodesic. Recall from
Proposition

F.10
that

\displaystyle(g^{(2n+1)}(0))^{ij}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\big(g^{(2n)}(0)\big)^{ij}

\displaystyle=C_{2n}\delta^{i\beta}T^{j}_{\beta,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{2n}},

for every
n\geq 0
and every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
, where

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta},

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\beta i_{1}i_{2}}

\displaystyle=R^{\alpha}_{\beta i_{1}i_{2}},

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\beta i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}

\displaystyle=R^{\alpha}_{\alpha_{1}i_{1}i_{2}}\dotsm R^{\alpha_{n-2}}_{\alpha_{n-1}i_{2n-3}i_{2n-2}}R^{\alpha_{n-1}}_{\beta i_{2n-1}i_{2n}},\quad n\geq 2,

(cf.
Proposition

F.9
) and

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle C_{2n}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}C_{2(n-m)}\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+3)},\quad n\geq 1.

In the following lemma, we obtain an upper bound for the absolute value of the coefficients
C_{2n}
.

Lemma F.26
.

Let
C_{2n}
be defined as above. Then

|C_{2n}|\leq(2n)!

for every
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

The result holds trivially for
C_{0}
. Let now
n\geq 1
and assume that
|C_{2k}|\leq(2k)!
for every
k<n
. Then

\displaystyle|C_{2n}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{n}{\binom{2n}{2m}}|C_{2(n-m)}|\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+3)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}{\binom{2n}{2m}}|C_{2(n-m)}|\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+3)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}{\binom{2n}{2m}}(2(n-m))!\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(2m+2)(2m+3)}

\displaystyle=2n!\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{(2m+1)2^{2m+1}}{(2m+3)!}

\displaystyle\leq 2n!

∎

Using this bound on the coefficients
C_{2n}
and the bound on the tensor
T
derived in
Eq.

F.16
, we can bound the norm of the derivatives of the gradient of
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
along
\gamma(t)
.

Lemma F.27
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of dimension
d
which is also a complete and connected symmetric space. Assume that there exists some constant
\mathbf{K}\geq 1
such that, for every
x\in M
, the coefficients of the Riemann curvature tensor in normal coordinates centered at
x
satisfy
|R_{ijk}^{l}(x)|\leq\mathbf{K}
for every
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
. Let
z\in M
be a fixed point, and let
x\in M
be outside the cut locus of
z
. Let
\gamma(t)=\exp_{z}(t\log^{M}_{z}x)
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d^{2n+1}}{dt^{2n+1}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\left.\frac{d^{2n}}{dt^{2n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))\Bigg|_{g}

\displaystyle\leq d^{3n+1}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2n)!r^{2n},

for every
n\geq 0
, where
r=d_{g}(x,z)
.

Proof.

Taking normal coordinates centered at
z
,

\left.\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))=(g^{(n)}(0))^{ij}\delta_{ik}(\text{lift}_{z}v)^{k}\partial_{j}.

Using the expression for the derivatives of the coefficients of the inverse metric in normal coordinates found in
Proposition

F.10
we know that

\left.\frac{d^{2n+1}}{dt^{2n+1}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))=(g^{(2n+1)}(0))^{ij}\delta_{ik}(\text{lift}_{z}v)^{k}\partial_{j}=0.

Also, from the bound on the coefficients
C_{2n}
shown in
Lemma

F.26
and the bound on the tensor
T
obtained in
Eq.

F.16
, we know that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big|\big(g^{(2n)}(0)\big)^{ij}\big|=|C_{2n}\delta^{i\beta}T^{j}_{\beta,i_{1},\dotsc,i_{2n}}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{2n}}|&\leq(2n)!d^{2n}d^{n-1}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n}\\
&=(2n)!d^{3n-1}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n},\end{split}

(F.26)

for every
n\geq 1
, and every
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
.

Therefore, since
|\textup{lift}_{z}v|_{g}=1
, we can use
Eq.

F.26
to conclude that for every
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\Bigg|\left.\frac{d^{2n}}{dt^{2n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))\Bigg|_{g}

\displaystyle=\big|(g^{(2n)}(0))^{ij}\delta_{ik}(\textup{lift}_{z}\,v)^{k}\partial_{j}\big|_{g}

\displaystyle=\Big(\delta_{\alpha\beta}(g^{(2n)}(0))^{i\alpha}\delta_{ik}(\textup{lift}_{z}\,v)^{k}(g^{(2n)}(0))^{j\beta}\delta_{jl}(\textup{lift}_{z}\,v)^{l}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(d(2n)!^{2}d^{6n}\mathbf{K}^{2n}r^{4n}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq d^{3n+1}\mathbf{K}^{n}(2n)!r^{2n}.

Lastly, for
n=0
note that
g^{ij}(\gamma(0))=\delta^{ij}
, and so

|\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(0))|_{g}\leq 1\leq d.

∎

With all of the above lemmas in mind, we can finally give a proof for
Lemma

F.22
.

Proof of
Lemma

F.22
.

Given
p\in B
and
z\in\pi^{-1}(p)
, recall from
Lemma

F.25
that

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}\geq\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z))\rangle_{g}-2|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}|u|_{g},

(F.27)

for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(\alpha,z)
, where
q=\pi(x)
.

Recall that

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)-\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left.\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!}\left.\frac{d^{2n}}{dt^{2n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t)),

where
\gamma(t)=\exp_{z}(t\log^{M}_{z}x)
, and where the last inequality follows from
Lemma

F.27
, as the odd derivatives vanish. Using the same lemma, we can bound the norm of
u
,

\displaystyle|u|_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!}\Bigg|\left.\frac{d^{2n}}{dt^{2n}}\right|_{t=0}\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(\gamma(t))\Bigg|_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\dim(M)^{3n+1}\mathbf{K}^{n}r^{2n}.

Lastly, recall that
r\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\dim(M)^{3/2}\mathbf{K}^{1/2}}
by assumption and so it holds that

|u|_{g}\leq\dim(M)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\dim(M)^{3}\mathbf{K}r^{2})^{n}=\frac{\dim(M)^{4}\mathbf{K}r^{2}}{1-\dim(M)^{3}\mathbf{K}r^{2}}\leq 2\dim(M)^{4}\mathbf{K}r^{2},

since
1-\dim(M)^{3}\mathbf{K}r^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Thus, we can lower bound the right-hand side of
Eq.

F.27
to conclude that

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}\geq\langle H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z))\rangle_{g}-4|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}\dim(M)^{4}\mathbf{K}r^{2}.

(F.28)

Let us now lower bound the first term on the right-hand side of
Eq.

F.28
. Considering normal coordinates centered at
z
, recall from the proof of
Lemma

F.27
that

\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)=g^{ij}(x)\delta_{ik}(\textup{lift}_{z}v)^{k}\partial_{j},

and so in particular

\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)=(\textup{lift}_{z}v)^{i}\partial_{i}.

Moreover, recall that

H^{M}_{z}(x)=\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\log^{M}_{z}x]=\left.\nabla_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,F\right|_{z},

and so writing
H^{M}_{z}(x)
in normal coordinates, since the Christoffel symbols and the derivatives of the coefficients of the inverse metric vanish at
z=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle H^{M}_{z}(x)=\left.\nabla_{\log^{M}_{z}x}\,\textup{grad}_{g}\,F\right|_{z}

\displaystyle=x^{l}\partial_{l}(\textup{grad}_{g}\,F)^{i}\partial_{i}

\displaystyle=x^{l}g^{ik}(0)\partial_{l}\partial_{k}F(0)\partial_{i}

\displaystyle=x^{l}\delta^{ik}\partial_{lk}F(0)\partial_{i}.

Using the expressions for the gradient of
\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}
and
H^{M}_{z}
in normal coordinates, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle-H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)\rangle_{g}&=-(H^{M}_{z}(x))^{i}\left(\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)\right)^{j}\delta_{ij}\\
&=-\delta^{ik}\partial_{kl}F(0)x^{l}(\textup{lift}_{z}\,v)^{j}\delta_{ij}\\
&=-\delta^{k}_{j}\partial_{kl}F(0)x^{l}(\textup{lift}_{z}\,v)^{j}\\
&=-\partial_{kl}F(0)x^{l}(\textup{lift}_{z}\,v)^{k}.\end{split}

(F.29)

Now, since
v
is the eigenvector associated with the minimum eigenvalue of
\nabla^{2}\tilde{F}(p)
, it holds that
\textup{lift}_{z}\,v
is an eigenvector associated with the minimum eigenvalue of
\nabla^{2}F(z)
. Denoting the eigenvalue associated with
v
as
\lambda_{v}
, it holds that

\nabla^{2}_{h}\,\tilde{F}(p)[v]=\lambda_{v}v,

so we can apply
Proposition

A.61
to conclude that

(\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\textup{lift}_{z}\,v])^{\mathcal{H}}=\textup{lift}_{z}\nabla^{2}_{h}\,\tilde{F}(p)[v]=\lambda_{v}\,\textup{lift}_{z}\,v.

Furthermore,

(\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\textup{lift}_{z}\,v])^{\mathcal{H}}=\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\textup{lift}_{z}\,v].

Using the symmetry of the Hessian and
Proposition

A.62
, we know that

(\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\textup{lift}_{z}\,v])^{\mathcal{V}}=\sum_{w\in V_{z}M}\langle\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\textup{lift}_{z}\,v],w\rangle_{g}\,w=\sum_{w\in V_{z}M}\langle\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[w],\textup{lift}_{z}v\rangle_{g}\,w=0,

where
V_{z}M
denotes the vertical tangent space of
M
at
z
. Thus,

\nabla^{2}_{g}\,F(z)[\textup{lift}_{z}\,v]=\lambda_{v}\,\textup{lift}_{z}\,v,

and so, since
\lambda_{v}\leq-\lambda_{*}
by
2.15
, we know that

\partial_{kl}F(0)(\textup{lift}_{z}v)^{k}\leq-\lambda_{*}\delta_{il}(\textup{lift}_{z}v)^{i},

so, we can lower bound
Eq.

F.29
as

\displaystyle\langle-H^{M}_{z}(x),\textup{grad}_{g}\,\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(z)\rangle_{g}=-\partial_{kl}F(0)x^{l}(\textup{lift}_{z}v)^{k}\geq\lambda_{*}\delta_{il}(\textup{lift}_{z}v)^{i}x^{l}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{*}\tilde{r}^{M}_{z,v}(x)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{*}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q),

where the last equality follows from
Proposition

F.21
. Inserting this inequality into
Eq.

F.28
we conclude that

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}\geq\lambda_{*}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)-4|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}\dim(M)^{4}\mathbf{K}r^{2}.

Lastly, note that

|H^{M}_{z}(x)|_{g}=|\nabla^{2}F(z)[\log_{z}x]|_{g}\leq\dim(M)A_{2}r,

where
A_{2}
is the Lipschitz constant of the gradient of
\tilde{F}
—which coincides with that of the gradient of
F
.

This allows us to conclude that

\langle H_{p}(q),\textup{P}^{-1}_{\log^{B}_{p}q}\,\textup{grad}_{h}\,\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)\rangle_{h}\geq\lambda_{*}\tilde{r}_{p,v}(q)-4\dim(M)^{5}\mathbf{K}A_{2}r^{3},

as desired.
∎
